Algebra 1 — Unit 6: Solving and Modeling with Quadratics — Canonical Bank

Teacher-facing canonical content factory. Secure items and solutions are included for authorized downstream assembly.

What's To Come — 6.1

U6-S1-WTC01

Solve $(x+15)(x-3)=0$ using the zero product property.
Before formal instruction, record one feature of the displayed representation that could help you test your conclusion.

Teacher answer / solution

$x=-15$ or $x=3$. A useful feature should be tied to the displayed graph or structure.

Set each factor equal to zero and solve the two linear equations.

What's To Come — 6.2

U6-S2-WTC01

Solve $x^2+10x=4$ by completing the square.
Before formal instruction, record one feature of the displayed representation that could help you test your conclusion.

Teacher answer / solution

$x=-5\pm\sqrt{29}$. A useful feature should be tied to the displayed graph or structure.

Add 25 to both sides: $(x+5)^2=29$. Take square roots: $x+5=\pm\sqrt{29}$, so $x=-5\pm\sqrt{29}$.

Notes Example — 6.2

What's To Come — 6.3

U6-S3-WTC01

For $x^2-5x-8=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.
Before formal instruction, record one feature of the displayed representation that could help you test your conclusion.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=57$; two distinct real solutions. A useful feature should be tied to the displayed graph or structure.

$b^2-4ac=(-5)^2-4(1)(-8)=57$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

Notes Example — 6.3

U6-S3-NOTE-EX01

For $x^2-5x+16=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=-39$; no real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(-5)^2-4(1)(16)=-39$, so the equation has no real solutions.

Notes You Try It — 6.3

U6-S3-NOTE-YTI01

What's To Come — 6.5

U6-S5-WTC01

A height model is $H(t)=-1(t-11)^2+37$. State the maximum height and the time when it occurs. Then find $H(13)$.
Before formal instruction, record one feature of the displayed representation that could help you test your conclusion.

Teacher answer / solution

Maximum 37 at $t=11$; $H(13)=33$. A useful feature should be tied to the displayed graph or structure.

The vertex is $(11,37)$ and the negative leading coefficient makes it a maximum. Substituting $t=13$ gives 33.

Notes Example — 6.5

U6-S5-NOTE-EX01

A height model is $H(t)=-2(t-9)^2+26$. State the maximum height and the time when it occurs. Then find $H(11)$.

Teacher answer / solution

Maximum 26 at $t=9$; $H(11)=18$.

The vertex is $(9,26)$ and the negative leading coefficient makes it a maximum. Substituting $t=11$ gives 18.

U6-S1-P1-R01

For $y=-(x+10)(x-1)$, state the zeros and the x-intercepts.

Teacher answer / solution

Zeros: $x=-10$ and $x=1$; x-intercepts: $(-10,0)$ and $(1,0)$.

Set each factor equal to zero. Each zero becomes an x-intercept on the graph.

U6-S1-P1-M01

The quadratic $y=-x^2+x+110$ has zeros $x=-10$ and $x=11$. Explain how those zeros connect to a factored form and the graph.

Teacher answer / solution

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+10)(x-11)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-10,0)$ and $(11,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+10)(x-11)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-10,0)$ and $(11,0)$.

U6-S1-P1-M02

The quadratic $y=x^2+8x-48$ has zeros $x=-12$ and $x=4$. Explain how those zeros connect to a factored form and the graph.

Teacher answer / solution

A matching factored form is $y=(x+12)(x-4)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-12,0)$ and $(4,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=(x+12)(x-4)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-12,0)$ and $(4,0)$.

U6-S2-P1-R01

Which form would reveal the vertex most directly for a quadratic with vertex $(-4,8)$: standard, factored, or vertex form? Write a matching vertex-form equation with leading coefficient 1.

Teacher answer / solution

Vertex form; $y=(x+4)^2+8$.

Vertex form $y=a(x-h)^2+k$ displays the vertex $(h,k)$ directly.

U6-S2-P1-M01

Start with $y=x^2$. Describe the transformations that produce $y=2(x-6)^2-4$, including the effect of the outside factor.

Teacher answer / solution

Shift horizontally by 6 unit(s) right, shift 4 unit(s) down, and apply an outside factor of 2.

Shift horizontally by 6 unit(s) right, shift 4 unit(s) down, and apply an outside factor of 2. The magnitude of the factor changes vertical scale.

U6-S2-P1-M02

Start with $y=x^2$. Describe the transformations that produce $y=-3(x+3)^2+10$, including the effect of the outside factor.

Teacher answer / solution

Shift horizontally by 3 unit(s) left, shift 10 unit(s) up, and apply an outside factor of -3.

Shift horizontally by 3 unit(s) left, shift 10 unit(s) up, and apply an outside factor of -3. The negative factor reflects the graph across the x-axis.

Practice Set 1 — 6.3

U6-S3-P1-I01

For $x^2-6x-3=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=48$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(-6)^2-4(1)(-3)=48$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-S3-P1-I02

U6-S3-P1-R01

Factor the greatest common factor from $40x^2+70x$.

Teacher answer / solution

$10x(4x^1+7)$.

Both terms share $10x$. Dividing each term by \$10x$ leaves \$4x^1+7$.

U6-S3-P2-I06

For $x^2+x-13=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=53$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(1)^2-4(1)(-13)=53$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-S3-P2-I07

For $x^2-6x+30=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=-84$; no real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(-6)^2-4(1)(30)=-84$, so the equation has no real solutions.

U6-S3-P2-I08

For $x^2+2x+12=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=-44$; no real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(2)^2-4(1)(12)=-44$, so the equation has no real solutions.

Practice Set 3 — 6.3

U6-S3-P3-I01

For $x^2+3x-14=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=65$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(3)^2-4(1)(-14)=65$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-S3-P3-I02

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=33$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(11)^2-4(1)(22)=33$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

$12x(8x^1+3)$.

U6-S4-CYU-R01

Factor $25x^2-64$.

Teacher answer / solution

$(5x-8)(5x+8)$.

This is a difference of squares: $A^2-B^2=(A-B)(A+B)$.

xy
0-5
19
227
329
Teacher answer / solution

Linear; constant first difference 9.

CYU — 6.5

U6-S5-CYU-I01

A height model is $H(t)=-4(t-9)^2+23$. State the maximum height and the time when it occurs. Then find $H(11)$.

Teacher answer / solution

Maximum 23 at $t=9$; $H(11)=7$.

The vertex is $(9,23)$ and the negative leading coefficient makes it a maximum. Substituting $t=11$ gives 7.

Teacher answer / solution

Maximum 21 at $t=11$; $H(13)=17$.

The vertex is $(11,21)$ and the negative leading coefficient makes it a maximum. Substituting $t=13$ gives 17.

U6-S5-CYU-R01

Factor $x^2+4x+3$ completely.

Teacher answer / solution

$(x+1)(x+3)$.

Find numbers with product 3 and sum 4: 1 and 3. Therefore $x^2+4x+3=(x+1)(x+3)$.

U6-S5-CYU-M01

Start with $y=x^2$. Describe the transformations that produce $y=-3(x-3)^2+4$, including the effect of the outside factor.

Teacher answer / solution

Shift horizontally by 3 unit(s) right, shift 4 unit(s) up, and apply an outside factor of -3.

Shift horizontally by 3 unit(s) right, shift 4 unit(s) up, and apply an outside factor of -3. The negative factor reflects the graph across the x-axis.

Solve $x^2+22x=3$ by completing the square.

  1. $x=-11\pm\sqrt{124}$.
  2. $x=11\pm\sqrt{124}$
  3. $x=-11\pm124$
  4. $x=-8$
Teacher answer / solution

$x=-11\pm\sqrt{124}$.

Add 121 to both sides: $(x+11)^2=124$. Take square roots: $x+11=\pm\sqrt{124}$, so $x=-11\pm\sqrt{124}$.

U6-S3-BLK-I01

For $x^2-15x-9=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

  1. Discriminant $=261$; two distinct real solutions.
  2. one repeated real solution
  3. no real solutions
  4. Alternative 3
Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=261$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(-15)^2-4(1)(-9)=261$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-S3-BLK-R01

Factor the greatest common factor from $80x^2+40x$.

  1. $8x(10x^1+5)$.
  2. $8(10x^2+5x)$
  3. $x(80x^1+5)$
  4. $80x(2+5)$
Teacher answer / solution

$8x(10x^1+5)$.

Both terms share $8x$. Dividing each term by \$8x$ leaves \$10x^1+5$.

  4. $77x(2+7)$
Teacher answer / solution

$7x(11x^1+7)$.

Both terms share $7x$. Dividing each term by \$7x$ leaves \$11x^1+7$.

U6-S3-BLK-M01

Expand and simplify $( 9x-4)( 8x+4)$.

  1. $72x^2+32x-16$
  2. $72x^2+4x+16$
  3. $72x^2+4x-16$.
  4. $17x^2-16$
Teacher answer / solution

$72x^2+4x-16$.

Warm-Up 2 — 6.3

U6-S3-WU2-I01

For $x^2+10x=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to describe the real solutions.

Teacher answer / solution

Discriminant $=100$; two distinct real solutions.

$b^2-4ac=(10)^2-4(1)(0)=100$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-S3-WU2-R02

Factor the greatest common factor from $36x^3+156x$.

Teacher answer / solution

$12x(3x^2+13)$.

Both terms share $12x$. Dividing each term by \$12x$ leaves \$3x^2+13$.

U6-S4-WU1-PK01

Factor $81x^2-64$.

Teacher answer / solution

$(9x-8)(9x+8)$.

This is a difference of squares: $A^2-B^2=(A-B)(A+B)$.

U6-S4-WU1-PK02

Factor $x^2+22x+121$.

Teacher answer / solution

$(x+11)^2$.

The first and last terms are squares and the middle term is $2(11)x$, so the expression is $(x+11)^2$.

Warm-Up 1 — 6.5

U6-S5-WU1-PK01

Factor $x^2+8x+16$ completely.

Teacher answer / solution

$(x+4)(x+4)$.

Find numbers with product 16 and sum 8: 4 and 4. Therefore $x^2+8x+16=(x+4)(x+4)$.

U6-S5-WU1-PK02

Factor $x^2+6x-55$ completely.

Teacher answer / solution

$(x-5)(x+11)$.

Find numbers with product -55 and sum 6: -5 and 11. Therefore $x^2+6x-55=(x-5)(x+11)$.

U6-S5-WU1-PK03

Teacher answer / solution

Shift horizontally by 8 unit(s) left, shift 3 unit(s) down, and apply an outside factor of -1.

Shift horizontally by 8 unit(s) left, shift 3 unit(s) down, and apply an outside factor of -1. The negative factor reflects the graph across the x-axis.

Warm-Up 2 — 6.5

U6-S5-WU2-I01

A height model is $H(t)=-1(t-4)^2+21$. State the maximum height and the time when it occurs. Then find $H(6)$.

Teacher answer / solution

Maximum 21 at $t=4$; $H(6)=17$.

The vertex is $(4,21)$ and the negative leading coefficient makes it a maximum. Substituting $t=6$ gives 17.

U6-S5-WU2-R02

Factor $x^2+10x+16$ completely.

Teacher answer / solution

$(x+8)(x+2)$.

Find numbers with product 16 and sum 10: 8 and 2. Therefore $x^2+10x+16=(x+8)(x+2)$.

U6-S1-ET-C-M02

The quadratic $y=-x^2+3x+108$ has zeros $x=-9$ and $x=12$. Explain how those zeros connect to a factored form and the graph.

Teacher answer / solution

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+9)(x-12)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-9,0)$ and $(12,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+9)(x-12)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-9,0)$ and $(12,0)$.

U6-REV-Q05

Solve $x^2+4x=11$ by completing the square.

Teacher answer / solution

$x=-2\pm\sqrt{15}$.

Add 4 to both sides: $(x+2)^2=15$. Take square roots: $x+2=\pm\sqrt{15}$, so $x=-2\pm\sqrt{15}$.

U6-REV-Q06

$b^2-4ac=(-4)^2-4(1)(-2)=24$, so the equation has two distinct real solutions.

U6-EXT03

U6-SUM-V2-Q10

The quadratic $y=2x^2+2x-84$ has zeros $x=-7$ and $x=6$. Explain how those zeros connect to a factored form and the graph.

  1. Vertex is $(-7,6)$
  2. Zeros are the y-intercepts
  3. A matching factored form is $y=2(x+7)(x-6)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-7,0)$ and $(6,0)$.
  4. Factored form cannot show intercepts
Teacher answer / solution

A matching factored form is $y=2(x+7)(x-6)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-7,0)$ and $(6,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=2(x+7)(x-6)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-7,0)$ and $(6,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+11)(x-10)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-11,0)$ and $(10,0)$.

  4. reflection only

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+6)(x-8)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-6,0)$ and $(8,0)$.

A matching factored form is $y=-(x+6)(x-8)$; the graph crosses or touches the x-axis at $(-6,0)$ and $(8,0)$.
